They’re not pleasant, and I don’t want them, but I don’t have the sense that someone else is putting them there or controlling them.\n\nI haven’t felt like an outside force is directing my mind or making me think things. It’s more that my own mind gets stuck in these painful loops, if that makes sense. But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. I’ll look at the same few lines over and over and still not be able to decide what I’m supposed to do next. Half an hour can go by and I’ve made no progress. It’s like the information is right in front of me, but my brain won’t organize it into steps.\n\nWith TV or reading, it’s similar. I’ll realize I haven’t absorbed anything. I’m looking at the page or the screen, but I’m not really following it. Then I get frustrated with myself, which makes it even harder to focus. It feels like I’m trying to think through fog.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like your thoughts are being broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think I’ve had that. I’ve worried about what people think of me, but I haven’t felt like my actual thoughts were being sent out somehow or that other people could hear them.

It’s more private than that, I guess. The thoughts stay in my own head, even if they’re loud to me. I don’t have the sense that they’re being broadcast or picked up by other people.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main reason I came in is that I can’t get out from under this feeling that I’ve failed. Failed as a father, especially, and just failed at being a useful person in general. It’s not just a passing thought, it sort of loops in my head over and over, like I keep replaying all the ways I think I’ve let people down, and then it turns into this heavier feeling that maybe I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel dramatic inside my head. It feels… convincing. Like some part of me has already decided I’m not worth much anymore, and I’m here because I don’t seem to be able to shake that on my own.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of failure?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now, I think. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I’d had hard days before that, of course, but this particular feeling—like I’ve failed, like I didn’t do enough or wasn’t enough—settled in after she left and just hasn’t really lifted. At first I thought maybe it was just the adjustment, you know, the house feeling different, her being far away, but it’s been weeks and it’s still there every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. I used to have things that I genuinely looked forward to, and now they just sort of sit there like reminders of a version of me I can’t quite get back to.\n\nI’ve had this model railway project for years, and normally I could lose an entire afternoon in it—laying track, fixing little details, painting things that nobody else would even notice. It used to calm me down, give me something to focus on. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table. I see them when I walk past, and instead of feeling any pull to work on them, I just think, “What’s the point?” Like even starting would take more from me than I have.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. That was a big thing with my daughter and me. We’d watch together, talk through the innings, complain about the bullpen, all of that. Even if she wasn’t there, I used to still put the game on because it felt connected to her somehow. But lately even opening a game on TV feels pointless. I’ll think about turning it on, and then I just don’t. It feels empty before I even try.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been sitting there with some clear plan in my head, but I’ve had these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could go to sleep and not have to face the next day, that would be a relief. And that scared me, because I don’t usually think like that—or I didn’t think I did.\n\nThe thing that really unsettled me was I caught myself looking over my finances, almost like I was trying to make sure things would be in order for someone else. I wasn’t telling myself that’s what I was doing at first, I was pretending it was just being responsible, but there was this feeling underneath it… like I was getting things ready in case I wasn’t around. And when I realized that, it honestly frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes… my appetite has definitely changed. I don’t really make proper meals most days anymore. It’s not even always that I feel strongly nauseous or anything like that—it’s more like food just doesn’t occur to me, or the idea of cooking feels like too many steps. I’ll go through most of the day and then realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten.\n\nAnd then when I do eat, it’s not much. Usually something like toast, or a bowl of cereal, something I don’t have to think about. I’ve noticed my belt is looser—or actually, I’ve had to tighten it a notch—so I guess I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been carefully tracking it. It just sort of crept up on me.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of failure and worthlessness been constant over the past month, or have you had any days where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been… pretty constant, honestly. I wish I could say there are clear breaks from it, but it’s like this thing running in the background all the time.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t shake this feeling that I’ve failed—especially as a father. I keep going back over things in my head, conversations, choices, times I wasn’t enough or didn’t do enough, and it just loops. Even when I try to tell myself that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really land. It feels like the “truth” is that I messed up in some fundamental way.\n\nAnd it’s not just fatherhood. It’s this broader feeling that I’m not useful anymore, like I don’t really contribute anything, like I’m just taking up space. I’ll have moments where I can distract myself briefly, maybe for a little while, but underneath it the same thought is there: I failed, I’m not worth much, and I don’t really deserve much of a future. It’s hard to admit that out loud, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of hearing voices or seeing things that other people don’t see.\n\nI’ve had a lot going on in my own head, but it feels like my thoughts, if that makes sense. They’re not coming from outside me, and I’m not hearing someone talk to me. And I haven’t seen anything unusual or felt like things were there that weren’t there.\n\nSometimes when I’m exhausted or just sitting alone too long, my mind can get… loud, I guess, but it’s not voices. It’s more like my own thoughts repeating. So no, I don’t think I’ve experienced anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of more than two weeks over the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has the feeling of failure and worthlessness been consistently present?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of two weeks where I felt better. Not really. There might be little moments where I’m distracted or where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but it hasn’t gone away.\n\nThat feeling is just… there. It’s like I keep circling back to the same conclusion, that I’ve failed as a father, and failed as a person who’s supposed to be useful or dependable. I’ll replay things in my head over and over, and even if part of me tries to argue with it, it doesn’t help much. It feels like the same loop starts again.\n\nAnd it gets to this point where I think, well, if that’s true, then I don’t really deserve much of a future. I know that probably sounds heavy, but that’s honestly been what it feels like. Not something that lifted for a couple weeks—more like something I’ve been carrying the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy is… it’s really low. Not just “I’m tired” in the normal sense, but like my body is heavy before I even start doing anything.\n\nEven simple things feel strangely hard. Like carrying a basket of laundry upstairs — that shouldn’t be a big thing, but it feels like I’m moving through wet cement. I’ll look at it and have to sort of talk myself into doing it, and then halfway up the stairs I feel completely drained. Or sorting the mail, which sounds ridiculous because it’s just paper, but even that can feel like too much effort, like my brain and body both don’t want to move.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really seem to matter how long I’ve been in bed. I can stay there for hours, or feel like I’ve rested, and still when I get up I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m dragging myself from one small task to the next. My movements feel slower, heavier… like everything takes more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, especially the moving part. I don’t know if I hear it in my own speech as clearly, but my body feels slowed down a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like even small things take this ridiculous amount of effort. Carrying laundry upstairs, for example — I know that should be ordinary, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement. I’ll be holding the basket and every step feels heavier than it should. Or even sorting the mail, just standing there looking at envelopes, I can feel myself moving slowly, almost like I have to force each little action.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really change just because I’ve been in bed a long time. I can spend hours there, and when I get up I still don’t feel rested or lighter. I just feel heavy and slow, like my body hasn’t caught up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience any pleasure at all, even from things that you normally enjoy, when you're thinking about your daughter or other positive aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I try to tell myself there should be pleasure there, especially with things connected to my daughter, because those used to be the bright spots. They weren’t just hobbies, they were sort of… ours, or at least they felt tied to who I was when I felt more like myself.\n\nLike the model railway — I used to really look forward to that. I’d spend time thinking about little details, where the track should go, how to make the scenery look right, which piece we might work on next. It sounds small, but it gave me this quiet satisfaction. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table. I’ll see them and think I should do something with them, maybe it would make me feel better, but then I just stare at it and it feels empty. Like the interest is gone before I even pick anything up.\n\nAnd Cubs games too. That used to be something I could count on, especially watching with my daughter or even just having that connection. Now even opening a game on TV feels pointless. I’ll think, “Maybe I should put it on,” and then the thought just kind of dies there. It doesn’t give me that pull anymore, that little spark of looking forward to it.\n\nSo when I think about positive things, or things I’m supposed to enjoy, I can recognize they matter, especially my daughter. But actually feeling pleasure from them… that’s been really hard. It’s more like I know what I used to feel than actually feeling it now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of how you would end your life if you decided to act on those feelings of not wanting to be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t gotten to the point of having a clear plan like, “I’m going to do this specific thing this specific way,” if that’s what you mean. But I don’t want to make it sound like it’s nothing, because it hasn’t felt like nothing.\n\nI’ve had these thoughts where it feels like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m actively doing something in that moment, more like… the idea of not having to keep going feels like relief, which is a frightening thing to admit out loud.\n\nAnd what scared me more was that I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was getting things ready for someone else. Like looking at accounts and thinking about whether things would be understandable, or organized, if I weren’t around. I didn’t sit down thinking, “This is what I’m doing,” but then I realized the direction my mind had gone, and it really scared me. It felt too close to… preparing, I guess. Not a method, exactly, but not just a passing thought either.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt completely free of these feelings of failure and worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a day that was completely free of it. There might be moments where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right at the front of my mind, but it’s still there underneath, like this constant background judgment.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this feeling that I’ve failed — especially as a father, and also just as a useful person. It loops in my head over and over, like I keep reviewing evidence against myself. I’ll think about things I should have done better, ways I should have been stronger or more present, and then it turns into this bigger conclusion that I’m just… not worth much.\n\nAnd it’s not like I can argue myself out of it for a whole day. Even if I try to remind myself of good things, or that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really stick. The feeling comes back and sits there. Some days it gets so heavy that I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, like I’ve already messed up the things that mattered most. So no, I wouldn’t say there have been days where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel awful sometimes, and repetitive, like I can’t get away from them, but they still feel like mine. They’re not pleasant, and I don’t want them, but I don’t have the sense that someone else is putting them there or controlling them.\n\nI haven’t felt like an outside force is directing my mind or making me think things. It’s more that my own mind gets stuck in these painful loops, if that makes sense. But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. I’ll look at the same few lines over and over and still not be able to decide what I’m supposed to do next. Half an hour can go by and I’ve made no progress. It’s like the information is right in front of me, but my brain won’t organize it into steps.\n\nWith TV or reading, it’s similar. I’ll realize I haven’t absorbed anything. I’m looking at the page or the screen, but I’m not really following it. Then I get frustrated with myself, which makes it even harder to focus. It feels like I’m trying to think through fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had that. I’ve worried about what people think of me, but I haven’t felt like my actual thoughts were being sent out somehow or that other people could hear them.\n\nIt’s more private than that, I guess. The thoughts stay in my own head, even if they’re loud to me. I don’t have the sense that they’re being broadcast or picked up by other people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more like I know what I used to feel than actually feeling it now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of how you would end your life if you decided to act on those feelings of not wanting to be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t gotten to the point of having a clear plan like, “I’m going to do this specific thing this specific way,” if that’s what you mean. But I don’t want to make it sound like it’s nothing, because it hasn’t felt like nothing.\n\nI’ve had these thoughts where it feels like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m actively doing something in that moment, more like… the idea of not having to keep going feels like relief, which is a frightening thing to admit out loud.\n\nAnd what scared me more was that I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was getting things ready for someone else. Like looking at accounts and thinking about whether things would be understandable, or organized, if I weren’t around. I didn’t sit down thinking, “This is what I’m doing,” but then I realized the direction my mind had gone, and it really scared me. It felt too close to… preparing, I guess. Not a method, exactly, but not just a passing thought either.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt completely free of these feelings of failure and worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a day that was completely free of it. They’re not coming from outside me, and I’m not hearing someone talk to me. Not really. There might be little moments where I’m distracted or where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but it hasn’t gone away.\n\nThat feeling is just… there. I don’t know if I hear it in my own speech as clearly, but my body feels slowed down a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like even small things take this ridiculous amount of effort. Carrying laundry upstairs, for example — I know that should be ordinary, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement. I’ll be holding the basket and every step feels heavier than it should. Like looking at accounts and thinking about whether things would be understandable, or organized, if I weren’t around. I didn’t sit down thinking, “This is what I’m doing,” but then I realized the direction my mind had gone, and it really scared me. It felt too close to… preparing, I guess. Not a method, exactly, but not just a passing thought either.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt completely free of these feelings of failure and worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a day that was completely free of it. There might be moments where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right at the front of my mind, but it’s still there underneath, like this constant background judgment.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this feeling that I’ve failed — especially as a father, and also just as a useful person. It loops in my head over and over, like I keep reviewing evidence against myself. I’ll think about things I should have done better, ways I should have been stronger or more present, and then it turns into this bigger conclusion that I’m just… not worth much.\n\nAnd it’s not like I can argue myself out of it for a whole day. Even if I try to remind myself of good things, or that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really stick. The feeling comes back and sits there. Some days it gets so heavy that I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, like I’ve already messed up the things that mattered most. So no, I wouldn’t say there have been days where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel awful sometimes, and repetitive, like I can’t get away from them, but they still feel like mine. They’re not pleasant, and I don’t want them, but I don’t have the sense that someone else is putting them there or controlling them.\n\nI haven’t felt like an outside force is directing my mind or making me think things. It’s more that my own mind gets stuck in these painful loops, if that makes sense. But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. I’ll look at the same few lines over and over and still not be able to decide what I’m supposed to do next. Half an hour can go by and I’ve made no progress. It’s like the information is right in front of me, but my brain won’t organize it into steps.\n\nWith TV or reading, it’s similar. I’ll realize I haven’t absorbed anything. I’m looking at the page or the screen, but I’m not really following it. Then I get frustrated with myself, which makes it even harder to focus. It feels like I’m trying to think through fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had that. I’ve worried about what people think of me, but I haven’t felt like my actual thoughts were being sent out somehow or that other people could hear them.\n\nIt’s more private than that, I guess. The thoughts stay in my own head, even if they’re loud to me. I don’t have the sense that they’re being broadcast or picked up by other people.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks would you say you've been feeling this way, with little to no improvement?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. I don’t know if I noticed it all at once, but looking back, that’s when everything seemed to drop off and it hasn’t really lifted since.\n\nThere might have been a day here or there where I thought, maybe I’m coming out of it a little, but it doesn’t last. It’s like the information is right in front of me, but my brain won’t organize it into steps.\n\nWith TV or reading, it’s similar. I’ll realize I haven’t absorbed anything. I’m looking at the page or the screen, but I’m not really following it. Then I get frustrated with myself, which makes it even harder to focus. It feels like I’m trying to think through fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had that. I’ve worried about what people think of me, but I haven’t felt like my actual thoughts were being sent out somehow or that other people could hear them.\n\nIt’s more private than that, I guess. The thoughts stay in my own head, even if they’re loud to me. I don’t have the sense that they’re being broadcast or picked up by other people.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks would you say you've been feeling this way, with little to no improvement?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. Maybe… four weeks or so. It started pretty clearly after my daughter left for college out of state. I don’t know if I noticed it all at once, but looking back, that’s when everything seemed to drop off and it hasn’t really lifted since.\n\nThere might have been a day here or there where I thought, maybe I’m coming out of it a little, but it doesn’t last. Overall, I’d say it’s been about a month with not much improvement.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure even when good things happen to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to look forward to the model railway and Cubs games with my daughter, but now the trains just sit half-built on the table and even opening a game on TV feels pointless.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s… that’s probably one of the worst parts, honestly. It’s not just that I don’t have the energy to do things. It’s that even things that used to feel good don’t really land anymore.

I used to really look forward to working on my model railway. I’d spend hours adjusting little details, getting the track aligned, painting tiny pieces, planning out the layout. It sounds silly maybe, but it used to give me this quiet satisfaction. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table. I see them every day, and I know part of me should want to finish them, but I just… don’t feel anything about it. Or I feel tired just looking at them.

And Cubs games were a big thing too, especially with my daughter. We’d watch together, talk about the lineup, complain about the bullpen, all of that. Even if they lost, it still felt like something we shared. Now I’ll think, maybe I should put the game on, and then even opening it on TV feels pointless. Like, what’s the use? I can’t get myself interested, and if I do turn it on, it just becomes noise in the room.

So yes, even when something should be pleasant, or used to be pleasant, it just feels flat. Almost like I remember that I’m supposed to enjoy it, but I can’t actually get to the feeling.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main reason I came in is that I can’t get out from under this feeling that I’ve failed. It feels… convincing. Like some part of me has already decided I’m not worth much anymore, and I’m here because I don’t seem to be able to shake that on my own.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of failure?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now, I think. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I’d had hard days before that, of course, but this particular feeling—like I’ve failed, like I didn’t do enough or wasn’t enough—settled in after she left and just hasn’t really lifted. Like if I could go to sleep and not have to face the next day, that would be a relief. Even when I try to tell myself that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really land. It feels like the “truth” is that I messed up in some fundamental way.\n\nAnd it’s not just fatherhood. It’s this broader feeling that I’m not useful anymore, like I don’t really contribute anything, like I’m just taking up space. I’ll have moments where I can distract myself briefly, maybe for a little while, but underneath it the same thought is there: I failed, I’m not worth much, and I don’t really deserve much of a future. It’s hard to admit that out loud, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of hearing voices or seeing things that other people don’t see.\n\nI’ve had a lot going on in my own head, but it feels like my thoughts, if that makes sense. They’re not coming from outside me, and I’m not hearing someone talk to me. And I haven’t seen anything unusual or felt like things were there that weren’t there.\n\nSometimes when I’m exhausted or just sitting alone too long, my mind can get… loud, I guess, but it’s not voices. It’s more like my own thoughts repeating. So no, I don’t think I’ve experienced anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of more than two weeks over the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has the feeling of failure and worthlessness been consistently present?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of two weeks where I felt better. Not really. There might be little moments where I’m distracted or where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but it hasn’t gone away.\n\nThat feeling is just… there. It’s like I keep circling back to the same conclusion, that I’ve failed as a father, and failed as a person who’s supposed to be useful or dependable. I’ll replay things in my head over and over, and even if part of me tries to argue with it, it doesn’t help much. It feels like the same loop starts again.\n\nAnd it gets to this point where I think, well, if that’s true, then I don’t really deserve much of a future. I’ll look at it and have to sort of talk myself into doing it, and then halfway up the stairs I feel completely drained. Or sorting the mail, which sounds ridiculous because it’s just paper, but even that can feel like too much effort, like my brain and body both don’t want to move.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really seem to matter how long I’ve been in bed. My movements feel slower, heavier… like everything takes more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, especially the moving part. I don’t know if I hear it in my own speech as clearly, but my body feels slowed down a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like even small things take this ridiculous amount of effort. Carrying laundry upstairs, for example — I know that should be ordinary, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement. I’ll be holding the basket and every step feels heavier than it should. Or even sorting the mail, just standing there looking at envelopes, I can feel myself moving slowly, almost like I have to force each little action.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really change just because I’ve been in bed a long time. I can spend hours there, and when I get up I still don’t feel rested or lighter. I just feel heavy and slow, like my body hasn’t caught up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience any pleasure at all, even from things that you normally enjoy, when you're thinking about your daughter or other positive aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I try to tell myself there should be pleasure there, especially with things connected to my daughter, because those used to be the bright spots. They weren’t just hobbies, they were sort of… ours, or at least they felt tied to who I was when I felt more like myself.\n\nLike the model railway — I used to really look forward to that. Like looking at accounts and thinking about whether things would be understandable, or organized, if I weren’t around. I didn’t sit down thinking, “This is what I’m doing,” but then I realized the direction my mind had gone, and it really scared me. It felt too close to… preparing, I guess. Not a method, exactly, but not just a passing thought either.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt completely free of these feelings of failure and worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a day that was completely free of it. There might be moments where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right at the front of my mind, but it’s still there underneath, like this constant background judgment.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this feeling that I’ve failed — especially as a father, and also just as a useful person. It loops in my head over and over, like I keep reviewing evidence against myself. I’ll think about things I should have done better, ways I should have been stronger or more present, and then it turns into this bigger conclusion that I’m just… not worth much.\n\nAnd it’s not like I can argue myself out of it for a whole day. Even if I try to remind myself of good things, or that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really stick. The feeling comes back and sits there. Some days it gets so heavy that I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, like I’ve already messed up the things that mattered most. So no, I wouldn’t say there have been days where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel awful sometimes, and repetitive, like I can’t get away from them, but they still feel like mine. They’re not pleasant, and I don’t want them, but I don’t have the sense that someone else is putting them there or controlling them.\n\nI haven’t felt like an outside force is directing my mind or making me think things. It’s more that my own mind gets stuck in these painful loops, if that makes sense. But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. So no, I don’t think I’ve experienced anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of more than two weeks over the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has the feeling of failure and worthlessness been consistently present?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of two weeks where I felt better. Not really. There might be little moments where I’m distracted or where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but it hasn’t gone away.\n\nThat feeling is just… there. It’s like I keep circling back to the same conclusion, that I’ve failed as a father, and failed as a person who’s supposed to be useful or dependable. I’ll replay things in my head over and over, and even if part of me tries to argue with it, it doesn’t help much. It feels like the same loop starts again.\n\nAnd it gets to this point where I think, well, if that’s true, then I don’t really deserve much of a future. I know that probably sounds heavy, but that’s honestly been what it feels like. Not something that lifted for a couple weeks—more like something I’ve been carrying the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy is… it’s really low. Not just “I’m tired” in the normal sense, but like my body is heavy before I even start doing anything.\n\nEven simple things feel strangely hard. Like carrying a basket of laundry upstairs — that shouldn’t be a big thing, but it feels like I’m moving through wet cement. I’ll look at it and have to sort of talk myself into doing it, and then halfway up the stairs I feel completely drained. Or sorting the mail, which sounds ridiculous because it’s just paper, but even that can feel like too much effort, like my brain and body both don’t want to move.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really seem to matter how long I’ve been in bed. I can stay there for hours, or feel like I’ve rested, and still when I get up I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m dragging myself from one small task to the next. My movements feel slower, heavier… like everything takes more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, especially the moving part. I don’t know if I hear it in my own speech as clearly, but my body feels slowed down a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like even small things take this ridiculous amount of effort. Carrying laundry upstairs, for example — I know that should be ordinary, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement. I’ll be holding the basket and every step feels heavier than it should. Or even sorting the mail, just standing there looking at envelopes, I can feel myself moving slowly, almost like I have to force each little action.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really change just because I’ve been in bed a long time. I can spend hours there, and when I get up I still don’t feel rested or lighter. I just feel heavy and slow, like my body hasn’t caught up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience any pleasure at all, even from things that you normally enjoy, when you're thinking about your daughter or other positive aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I try to tell myself there should be pleasure there, especially with things connected to my daughter, because those used to be the bright spots. They weren’t just hobbies, they were sort of… ours, or at least they felt tied to who I was when I felt more like myself.\n\nLike the model railway — I used to really look forward to that. I’d spend time thinking about little details, where the track should go, how to make the scenery look right, which piece we might work on next. It sounds small, but it gave me this quiet satisfaction. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table. I’ll see them and think I should do something with them, maybe it would make me feel better, but then I just stare at it and it feels empty. Like the interest is gone before I even pick anything up.\n\nAnd Cubs games too. That used to be something I could count on, especially watching with my daughter or even just having that connection. Now even opening a game on TV feels pointless. I’ll think, “Maybe I should put it on,” and then the thought just kind of dies there. It doesn’t give me that pull anymore, that little spark of looking forward to it.\n\nSo when I think about positive things, or things I’m supposed to enjoy, I can recognize they matter, especially my daughter. But actually feeling pleasure from them… that’s been really hard. It’s more like I know what I used to feel than actually feeling it now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of how you would end your life if you decided to act on those feelings of not wanting to be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t gotten to the point of having a clear plan like, “I’m going to do this specific thing this specific way,” if that’s what you mean. But I don’t want to make it sound like it’s nothing, because it hasn’t felt like nothing.\n\nI’ve had these thoughts where it feels like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m actively doing something in that moment, more like… the idea of not having to keep going feels like relief, which is a frightening thing to admit out loud.\n\nAnd what scared me more was that I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was getting things ready for someone else. Like looking at accounts and thinking about whether things would be understandable, or organized, if I weren’t around. I didn’t sit down thinking, “This is what I’m doing,” but then I realized the direction my mind had gone, and it really scared me. It felt too close to… preparing, I guess. Not a method, exactly, but not just a passing thought either.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt completely free of these feelings of failure and worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a day that was completely free of it. There might be moments where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right at the front of my mind, but it’s still there underneath, like this constant background judgment.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this feeling that I’ve failed — especially as a father, and also just as a useful person. It loops in my head over and over, like I keep reviewing evidence against myself. I’ll think about things I should have done better, ways I should have been stronger or more present, and then it turns into this bigger conclusion that I’m just… not worth much.\n\nAnd it’s not like I can argue myself out of it for a whole day. Even if I try to remind myself of good things, or that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really stick. The feeling comes back and sits there. Some days it gets so heavy that I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, like I’ve already messed up the things that mattered most. So no, I wouldn’t say there have been days where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel awful sometimes, and repetitive, like I can’t get away from them, but they still feel like mine. They’re not pleasant, and I don’t want them, but I don’t have the sense that someone else is putting them there or controlling them.\n\nI haven’t felt like an outside force is directing my mind or making me think things. It’s more that my own mind gets stuck in these painful loops, if that makes sense. But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. I’ll look at the same few lines over and over and still not be able to decide what I’m supposed to do next. Half an hour can go by and I’ve made no progress. It’s like the information is right in front of me, but my brain won’t organize it into steps.\n\nWith TV or reading, it’s similar. I’ll realize I haven’t absorbed anything. I’m looking at the page or the screen, but I’m not really following it. Then I get frustrated with myself, which makes it even harder to focus. The thoughts stay in my own head, even if they’re loud to me. Even when I try to tell myself that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really land. It feels like the “truth” is that I messed up in some fundamental way.\n\nAnd it’s not just fatherhood. It’s this broader feeling that I’m not useful anymore, like I don’t really contribute anything, like I’m just taking up space. I’ll have moments where I can distract myself briefly, maybe for a little while, but underneath it the same thought is there: I failed, I’m not worth much, and I don’t really deserve much of a future. It’s hard to admit that out loud, but that’s where my mind keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of hearing voices or seeing things that other people don’t see.\n\nI’ve had a lot going on in my own head, but it feels like my thoughts, if that makes sense. They’re not coming from outside me, and I’m not hearing someone talk to me. And I haven’t seen anything unusual or felt like things were there that weren’t there.\n\nSometimes when I’m exhausted or just sitting alone too long, my mind can get… loud, I guess, but it’s not voices. It’s more like my own thoughts repeating. So no, I don’t think I’ve experienced anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of more than two weeks over the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has the feeling of failure and worthlessness been consistently present?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch of two weeks where I felt better. Not really. There might be little moments where I’m distracted or where I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but it hasn’t gone away.\n\nThat feeling is just… there. It’s like I keep circling back to the same conclusion, that I’ve failed as a father, and failed as a person who’s supposed to be useful or dependable. I’ll replay things in my head over and over, and even if part of me tries to argue with it, it doesn’t help much. It feels like the same loop starts again.\n\nAnd it gets to this point where I think, well, if that’s true, then I don’t really deserve much of a future. I know that probably sounds heavy, but that’s honestly been what it feels like. Not something that lifted for a couple weeks—more like something I’ve been carrying the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes. My energy is… it’s really low. Not just “I’m tired” in the normal sense, but like my body is heavy before I even start doing anything.\n\nEven simple things feel strangely hard. Like carrying a basket of laundry upstairs — that shouldn’t be a big thing, but it feels like I’m moving through wet cement. I’ll look at it and have to sort of talk myself into doing it, and then halfway up the stairs I feel completely drained. Or sorting the mail, which sounds ridiculous because it’s just paper, but even that can feel like too much effort, like my brain and body both don’t want to move.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really seem to matter how long I’ve been in bed. I can stay there for hours, or feel like I’ve rested, and still when I get up I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m dragging myself from one small task to the next. My movements feel slower, heavier… like everything takes more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, especially the moving part. I don’t know if I hear it in my own speech as clearly, but my body feels slowed down a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like even small things take this ridiculous amount of effort. Carrying laundry upstairs, for example — I know that should be ordinary, but it feels like I’m pushing through wet cement. I’ll be holding the basket and every step feels heavier than it should. Or even sorting the mail, just standing there looking at envelopes, I can feel myself moving slowly, almost like I have to force each little action.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really change just because I’ve been in bed a long time. I can spend hours there, and when I get up I still don’t feel rested or lighter. I just feel heavy and slow, like my body hasn’t caught up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience any pleasure at all, even from things that you normally enjoy, when you're thinking about your daughter or other positive aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I try to tell myself there should be pleasure there, especially with things connected to my daughter, because those used to be the bright spots. They weren’t just hobbies, they were sort of… ours, or at least they felt tied to who I was when I felt more like myself.\n\nLike the model railway — I used to really look forward to that. I’d spend time thinking about little details, where the track should go, how to make the scenery look right, which piece we might work on next. It sounds small, but it gave me this quiet satisfaction. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table. I’ll see them and think I should do something with them, maybe it would make me feel better, but then I just stare at it and it feels empty. Like the interest is gone before I even pick anything up.\n\nAnd Cubs games too. That used to be something I could count on, especially watching with my daughter or even just having that connection. Now even opening a game on TV feels pointless. I’ll think, “Maybe I should put it on,” and then the thought just kind of dies there. It doesn’t give me that pull anymore, that little spark of looking forward to it.\n\nSo when I think about positive things, or things I’m supposed to enjoy, I can recognize they matter, especially my daughter. But actually feeling pleasure from them… that’s been really hard. It’s more like I know what I used to feel than actually feeling it now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of how you would end your life if you decided to act on those feelings of not wanting to be alive?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t gotten to the point of having a clear plan like, “I’m going to do this specific thing this specific way,” if that’s what you mean. But I don’t want to make it sound like it’s nothing, because it hasn’t felt like nothing.\n\nI’ve had these thoughts where it feels like it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m actively doing something in that moment, more like… the idea of not having to keep going feels like relief, which is a frightening thing to admit out loud.\n\nAnd what scared me more was that I caught myself going over my finances almost like I was getting things ready for someone else. Like looking at accounts and thinking about whether things would be understandable, or organized, if I weren’t around. I didn’t sit down thinking, “This is what I’m doing,” but then I realized the direction my mind had gone, and it really scared me. It felt too close to… preparing, I guess. Not a method, exactly, but not just a passing thought either.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt completely free of these feelings of failure and worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a day that was completely free of it. There might be moments where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right at the front of my mind, but it’s still there underneath, like this constant background judgment.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to shake this feeling that I’ve failed — especially as a father, and also just as a useful person. It loops in my head over and over, like I keep reviewing evidence against myself. I’ll think about things I should have done better, ways I should have been stronger or more present, and then it turns into this bigger conclusion that I’m just… not worth much.\n\nAnd it’s not like I can argue myself out of it for a whole day. Even if I try to remind myself of good things, or that maybe I’m being too harsh, it doesn’t really stick. The feeling comes back and sits there. Some days it gets so heavy that I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, like I’ve already messed up the things that mattered most. So no, I wouldn’t say there have been days where I felt free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel awful sometimes, and repetitive, like I can’t get away from them, but they still feel like mine. They’re not pleasant, and I don’t want them, but I don’t have the sense that someone else is putting them there or controlling them.\n\nI haven’t felt like an outside force is directing my mind or making me think things. It’s more that my own mind gets stuck in these painful loops, if that makes sense. But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. I’ll look at the same few lines over and over and still not be able to decide what I’m supposed to do next. Half an hour can go by and I’ve made no progress. It’s like the information is right in front of me, but my brain won’t organize it into steps.\n\nWith TV or reading, it’s similar. I’ll realize I haven’t absorbed anything. I’m looking at the page or the screen, but I’m not really following it. Then I get frustrated with myself, which makes it even harder to focus. It feels like I’m trying to think through fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had that. I’ve worried about what people think of me, but I haven’t felt like my actual thoughts were being sent out somehow or that other people could hear them.\n\nIt’s more private than that, I guess. The thoughts stay in my own head, even if they’re loud to me. I don’t have the sense that they’re being broadcast or picked up by other people.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks would you say you've been feeling this way, with little to no improvement?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. I don’t know if I noticed it all at once, but looking back, that’s when everything seemed to drop off and it hasn’t really lifted since.\n\nThere might have been a day here or there where I thought, maybe I’m coming out of it a little, but it doesn’t last. Yes… yes, that’s exactly it. Even if someone tries to remind me why I used to like something, it doesn’t really get through.

Like with the model railway — I used to genuinely look forward to it. I’d think about little changes I wanted to make, or I’d notice some tiny detail that needed fixing and that would pull me in for hours. Now it’s just sitting there half-built on the table. I see it, and I know I used to care about it, but that feeling just isn’t there. If someone said, “Why don’t you work on it a little, maybe it’ll help,” I can understand what they mean, but it doesn’t make me want to do it. It just feels like a chore, or like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby.

Same with Cubs games. Watching them with my daughter used to be something I really enjoyed — not just the game, but the routine of it, talking about it, having that shared thing. Now even turning the game on feels pointless. I’ll think, “Maybe I should watch, maybe that would be nice,” but then I just sit there and can’t make myself care. Even if someone tried to point out that it used to make me happy, I’d believe them, but I wouldn’t feel it. It’s like the positive side is there in theory, but I can’t react to it.

But no, not like someone or something is controlling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating on things like reading or watching TV over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s actually one of the things that’s been hard to explain, because I used to be able to think through complicated things without even really noticing I was doing it.\n\nI mean, I used to manage engineering spreadsheets — lots of numbers, formulas, cross-checking things, keeping track of details — and that was just… normal for me. Not effortless exactly, but I knew how to do it. My mind could hold the pieces together.\n\nLately, though, I’ll sit with something simple, like a grocery list or a bill, and I just stare at it. It feels like I’m trying to think through fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are being broadcasted so that others can hear them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had that. Overall, I’d say it’s been about a month with not much improvement.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience any pleasure even when good things happen to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Almost like I remember that I’m supposed to enjoy it, but I can’t actually get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not under your control?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure what you mean by “not under my control.” If you mean like my thoughts are being controlled by someone else, or put into my head somehow, then no, I don’t think so. But it still feels like they’re my thoughts, if that makes sense. Not like some outside force is controlling them. Just… more like I can’t get them to quiet down or move on when I want them to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to react to normally pleasurable activities even when someone tries to encourage you or help you see the positive side?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly it.
